For Newton Matching, setting f = ˜ rρ in the covariance–gradient identity yields covariance-form and gradient-form sample-wise targets with the same conditional mean and hence the same population minimizer under squared-loss regression. Their difference is a zero-mean control variate, so any matrix-weighted affine combination of the two remains population-exact. When the required conditional second moments exist, the matrix weight can be chosen to minimize the variance. Appendix E.1 first introduces the Stein identity for the Langevin Stein operator. Ap-pendix E.2 then uses this identity to construct a family of zero-mean Stein control variates. By specializing the construction to the posterior pρ

> 1|t

(·| xt), Appendix E.3 defines posterior Stein kernels and proves the covariance–gradient identity displayed above. Building on the adjoint calculus in Appendix D.6, Appendix E.4 constructs the posterior sensitivity kernel and its adjoint-based evaluation used by exact Newton Matching in Section 8. Finally, Appendix E.5 records the Gaussian Stein kernels and posterior-moment formulas used by the Gaussian-kernel approximation in Section 9. As in Appendix D, all conclusions in this appendix apply to both the standard flow matching construction and the one-sided interpolant setting. The extension of Newton Matching to one-sided interpolants is discussed in Appendix H.2. 

E.1 Langevin Stein Identities 

In this subsection, we introduce the Langevin Stein operator [16, 55] and its columnwise extension to matrix-valued test fields. The Stein identity provides the zero-mean property used to construct control variates, while the product rule, combined with the defining relation of a posterior Stein kernel, yields the covariance–gradient identity. 

Definition 117. Let p be a positive C1 density on Rd, and let ϕ : Rd → Rd be a continuously differentiable vector field. The Langevin Stein operator associated with p is 

Spϕ : Rd → R, (Spϕ)( x) := ∇ · ϕ(x) + ϕ(x)⊤∇ log p(x).

Let Φ : Rd → Rd×d be C1, and write Φ = [ ϕ1, . . . , ϕ d], where each column ϕj : Rd → Rd

is C1. Define the Langevin Stein operator for the matrix-valued test field Φ by applying Sp

columnwise: 

SpΦ : Rd → Rd, (SpΦ)( x) := 



(Spϕ1)( x)...(Spϕd)( x)

 = ∇ · Φ( x) + Φ( x)⊤∇ log p(x),

174 where ∇ · Φ( x) ∈ Rd denotes the columnwise divergence: 

 ∇ · Φ( x)

=

> d

X

∂Φij (x)

∂x i

, j = 1 , . . . , d. 

Under the appropriate integrability and boundary conditions, the Langevin Stein oper-ator yields the following zero-mean property, which is called the Stein identity. 

Lemma 118. Use the notation in Definition 117. Assume that 

EX∼p [|(Spϕ)( X)|] < ∞, (203) 

and that the boundary term vanishes: 

lim 

> R→∞

Z

> ∂B R

p(x)ϕ(x)⊤n(x)d S(x) = 0 , (204) 

where BR := {x : ∥x∥2 ≤ R} and n(x) is the outward unit normal vector on ∂B R. Then 

EX∼p [( Spϕ)( X)] = 0 . (205) 

Consequently, if every column of Φ : Rd → Rd×d satisfies (203) and (204) , then 

EX∼p [( SpΦ)( X)] = 0 ∈ Rd. (206) 

Proof Since p is positive and continuously differentiable, 

p(x)( Spϕ)( x) = p(x)∇ · ϕ(x) + ϕ(x)⊤∇p(x) = ∇ · (p(x)ϕ(x)) .

Integrating over BR and applying the divergence theorem gives 

Z

(Spϕ)( x)p(x)d x =

Z

> ∂B R

p(x)ϕ(x)⊤n(x)d S(x).

By (203) and (204), taking R → ∞ proves (205). Applying (205) to every column of Φ gives (206). In all subsequent applications, the densities and test fields are assumed to satisfy the integrability and boundary conditions in (203) and (204). Under these conditions, the Langevin Stein operator applied to an admissible test field has zero expectation and can therefore serve as a control variate. We next record the product rule for the Langevin Stein operator. 

Lemma 119. Let f : Rd → R be C1. For a vector field ϕ : Rd → Rd,

Sp(f ϕ )( x) = ϕ(x)⊤∇f (x) + f (x)( Spϕ)( x). (207) 

For a matrix-valued test function Φ : Rd → Rd×d,

Sp(f Φ)( x) = Φ( x)⊤∇f (x) + f (x)( SpΦ)( x).

175 Proof For the vector-field identity, 

Sp(f ϕ )( x) = ∇ · (f (x)ϕ(x)) + f (x)ϕ(x)⊤∇ log p(x)= ϕ(x)⊤∇f (x) + f (x)∇ · ϕ(x) + f (x)ϕ(x)⊤∇ log p(x)= ϕ(x)⊤∇f (x) + f (x)( Spϕ)( x).

The matrix-valued identity follows by applying (207) to every column of Φ.

In either case, the pathwise adjoint is obtained by solving the ODE (196) along the sampled path. 180 E.5 Gaussian Stein Kernels and Posterior Moments 

The Gaussian-kernel approximation in Section 9.2.2 uses the covariance matrix of a Gaussian surrogate in place of the exact posterior sensitivity kernel constructed above. This subsection first records the Gaussian Stein identity, which identifies this covariance matrix as an exact Stein kernel for the surrogate distribution, and then derives exact formulas for the mean and covariance of the interpolant posterior that can be used to specify the surrogate. Let X ∼ N (m, Σ), where m ∈ Rd and Σ ∈ Sd

> ++

. For every sufficiently regular function 

f : Rd → R, the Gaussian Stein identity states that 

EX∼N (m, Σ) [( X − m)f (X)] = Σ EX∼N (m, Σ) [∇f (X)] .

Equivalently, the constant matrix field x 7 → Σ is a Stein kernel for N (m, Σ). This property is specific to Gaussian distributions: for a non-Gaussian posterior, its covariance matrix is generally not a posterior Stein kernel. This observation motivates approximating the posterior by a Gaussian for computational convenience and using the corresponding Stein kernel. Consider a Gaussian surrogate ˆpρ

> 1|t

(·| xt) := N



where cM ρt (xt) ∈ Rd and bΣρt (xt) ∈ Sd 

> ++

may be supplied by a trained model, estimated via statistical tools, or specified analytically. Its score is given by 

∇x1 log ˆ pρ

> 1|t

(x1|xt) = −bΣρt (xt)−1 

x1 − cM ρt (xt)



.

Hence, the matrix field

> 1|t

(·| xt), since 



# F Proofs for Approximate Newton Matching 

This appendix provides the proofs of the results in Section 9. We use the notation, targets, and assumptions introduced there and follow the same organization. Appendix F.1 characterizes the population-stationary points of the two covariance approximations in Sec-tion 9.1 and establishes their critical-point consistency. Appendix F.2 proves the critical-point consistency for the reference-adjoint and Gaussian-kernel approximations in Sec-tion 9.2. Finally, Appendix F.3 establishes the decomposition underlying approximate reg-ularization and proves the reward-ascent and limiting results for the unregularized iteration in Section 9.3.

Proposition 43. Consider the terminal density induced by (127) , i.e., 

q = T (vρ + ηΓρ,r ) .

We have the reward-ascent certificate 

EX∼q [r(X)] ≥ EX∼ρ [r(X)] .

Furthermore, whenever 

η > 0, r̸ ≡ const ,

the reward increases strictly: 

EX∼q [r(X)] > EX∼ρ [r(X)] .

Consequently, if the reward is nonconstant, then the correction Γρ,r is not critical-point consistent for π.

Proof The value-ascent certificate is a specialization of Theorem 12 with f := r. If ρ = π

but r is nonconstant, then the strict value ascent implies that q̸ = ρ; hence, Γ ρ,r ̸ = 0 even at ρ = π. Therefore, the approximation is not critical-point consistent.

To prove Proposition 45, we separate the terminal-density error into two components: a residual exponential tilt caused by the mismatch ˆ τ −τ , and an accumulated path-dissipation correction. The former can be controlled uniformly for bounded rewards through Hoeffding’s lemma [57, Equation (4.16)].

# G Canonical Updates with Time-Dependent Stepsizes 

Section 5 studies the finite-stepsize canonical retraction. The corresponding tangential update is ¯vt = vρt + ηΓρ,f t , η ∈ (0 , ∞) is constant .

Since ηΓρ,f ∈ Tvρ Vcan , composing this velocity-space addition with the canonical projection implements the canonical retraction introduced in Section 5.1. This appendix replaces the constant stepsize η by a time-dependent modulation: ¯vt = vρt + ηtΓρ,f t , η ∈ C1([0 , 1]; (0 , ∞)) is time-dependent .

Although Γ ρ,f is a tangent vector in Tvρ Vcan , its time-dependent modulation 



ηtΓρ,f t



> t∈[0 ,1]

need not belong to Tvρ Vcan . The resulting velocity-space addition followed by canonical-ization is therefore generally not a retraction in the geometric sense. Nevertheless, the resulting velocity field generates a well-defined probability path and terminal density. We derive an exact formula for this terminal density in Appendix G.1. Then, Appendix G.2 shows that the monotonicity condition ˙ηt ≥ 0, t ∈ (0 , 1) ,

is sufficient to extend the finite-stepsize value-ascent certificate in Section 5.2 to this new setting. Furthermore, if we set f = ˜ rρ and use a nondecreasing η ∈ C1([0 , 1]; (0 , τ ]) for Newton Matching, the reverse-KL descent guarantee remains valid. 192 G.1 Terminal-Density Characterization 

Since the canonical projection preserves terminal densities, the terminal density of the canonical updates with time-dependent stepsizes is determined entirely by the ambient velocity field before canonicalization. We therefore analyze the density path generated by ¯vρ,η,f t = vρt + ηtΓρ,f t ,

and recover the same terminal density as the field obtained after canonicalization. This subsection derives the resulting terminal-density formula in two steps. First, Ap-pendix G.1.1 establishes a general terminal-density formula for velocity-space additive up-dates with generic gradient fields: ¯vρ, Ψ 

> t

= vρt + κt∇Ψt.

This calculation uses neither the posterior-value structure nor tangency to the canonical manifold. Appendix G.1.2 then chooses Ψ t = ηtV ρt [f ]. This specialization provides the terminal-density formula for the update ¯ vρ,η,f t = vρt + ηtΓρ,f t .

G.2 Monotonicity Guarantees 

Appendix G.1 presents the terminal-density characterization for general additive up-dates with time-dependent stepsizes. We now identify conditions under which the mono-tonicity guarantees for constant stepsizes extend to this setting. A nondecreasing time-dependent stepsize yields value ascent for general f ; for f = ˜ rρ, the additional condition 

η1 ≤ τ yields reverse-KL descent. Appendix G.2.1 first derives an identity for the expectation of the pathwise correction under the updated density and uses it to prove value ascent. Appendix G.2.2 then special-izes to f = ˜ rρ, yielding the time-dependent-stepsize counterparts of the Newton Matching terminal-density formula and three-point identity. Under the additional bound η1 ≤ τ , the latter implies one-stage reverse-KL descent. 

G.2.1 Value-Ascent Certificate 

The terminal-density formula in Appendix G.1.2 contains a pathwise correction that need not be nonnegative pointwise. To establish value ascent, it suffices to show that its expectation under the updated density q is nonnegative. We first derive an exact identity for this expectation and then combine it with the terminal-density formula to obtain the time-dependent-stepsize counterpart of the decomposition in Section 5.2.

# H Extensions to Alternative Coordinates and Interpolants 

This appendix develops two complementary representation-level extensions of Newton Matching. Appendix H.1 changes the prediction coordinate: it rewrites regression objec-tives for exact and approximate tangential updates and for canonicalization in score and drift coordinates. Appendix H.2 instead shows that Newton Matching extends beyond the standard CFM construction adopted in the main text, which uses a Gaussian source and a linear interpolant. As a representative alternative, it develops the corresponding canoni-cal representations and regression objectives for a one-sided interpolant with deterministic initial state X0 = 0. In both extensions, the density-space objective, Fisher–Rao geome-try, and Newton direction remain unchanged; only the prediction coordinate or interpolant construction used to realize a Newton Matching stage is modified. 

H.1 Newton Matching under Score and Drift Coordinates

Developing this viewpoint into a complete score-space formulation, including an appropriate canonical man-ifold, transported density-space geometry, canonical retraction, Newton direction, and con-vergence theory, would require further careful analysis. We leave this extension, together with its drift-coordinate analogue, to future work.

H.2 Newton Matching under One-Sided Interpolants 

In this subsection, we extend Newton Matching beyond the standard CFM construction adopted in the main text, which uses a Gaussian source and a linear interpolant. Specifically, we consider a one-sided interpolant [58] with deterministic initial state X0 = 0. For suitable interpolant schedules, this one-sided construction also admits a Schr¨ odinger half-bridge interpretation with a linear-Gaussian reference [59]. The constructions in Appendices D and E remain applicable in this setting. 

H.2.1 Canonical Velocity, Score, and Drift 

The linear interpolant Xt = αtX1 + βtX0 in this paper is associated with a standard Gaussian source and the interpolant schedule satisfies the boundary conditions α0 = 0, 

β0 = 1, α1 = 1, and β1 = 0. For the one-sided interpolant considered here, the source is instead the deterministic endpoint X0 = 0. Thus, for a terminal density ρ ∈ P, we consider 

Xt = eαtX1 + eβtZ, X1 ∼ ρ, Z ∼ N (0 , I ), (239) 206 with the boundary conditions 

eα0 = 0 , eβ0 = 0 , eα1 = 1 , eβ1 = 0 .

I.1 Tilt Matching 

Explicit Tilt Matching (ETM) and Implicit Tilt Matching (ITM) are organized along the same exponential-tilt continuation path, but differ in the population-level update realized at each stage. Following the notation of Section 10.1, we relabel the current continuation density, its canonical velocity, and the inverse-temperature increment by ρbase , vbase =

C(ρbase ), and τ , respectively. We analyze the two methods separately.
